  <div class="lore-page">
    <h2>📜 The Float Knights Codex</h2>
    <div class="sub">A semi-official archive of the league's history, characters, ephemera, and unsolved mysteries. Maintained by various deeply unqualified people. Not legally admissible.</div>

    <div class="lore-subnav">
      <button class="lore-tab active" data-loretab="histories">Histories</button>

    <!-- ===== HISTORIES ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section active" data-loresection="histories">
      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Founding (Brewer's Reach Pitch, Boston)</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · league archives, partial · author unattributed</div>
        <p>Boston had a problem and Boston had a solution. The problem was that the Beavers' engineering guild kept resolving labor disputes by punching each other. The solution was an open-air pitch on the salt-cured grass at the edge of Brewer's Reach, where the punching could be organized, scored, ticketed, and — eventually — broadcast.</p>
        <p>The first match was held in the spring of League Year 1. Two squads of five Beavers, one balloon each, no weapons. By the third round both squads had switched gear with each other twice and a single popped balloon was ricocheting off the dam-bracing.</p>
        <p>Old Bristlebeard the First was on the field that day. He survived the inaugural match and went on to die in the championship final five years later, taking three Beavers with him. His Riot Shield is, at time of writing, still in circulation. It has since been carried by seven knights across six seasons. The League Office considers retiring it but does not, because every time the Office tries to retire it, it gets stolen back into circulation by a Raccoon.</p>
        <p>The lobster, by the way, was always there. The Beavers fed the original spectators between rounds because the only food vendor in walking distance sold lobster rolls and refused to leave. The tradition stuck. Lobster Reach Pitch is the unofficial name for the Brewer's Reach Pitch concession area to this day.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "The Beaver Years," chapter 1, League Library, Boston</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Collapse and the League's Response</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · "what every Knight should know," league orientation pamphlet</div>
        <p>You have been told about the Collapse. Maybe by your parents. Maybe by a neighbor who lost a city. Some of you grew up on a Collapse story; some of you never asked because you didn't want the answer. The League does not require you to have an opinion on the Collapse to play.</p>
        <p>Here is what the League will tell you, and only this:</p>
        <p>One night, the lights went out across most of the continent. The grids did not come back the next morning. The banks did not, either. Telecommunications survived in patches. Cities that survived survived because they reorganized around <em>something they could rally around in the absence of normal civic life</em>. Boston rallied around its engineers. Tampa rallied around its salvage crews. Reno rallied around its remaining slot machines, which were the only things in town that still worked, and that's how Reno still has neon.</p>
        <p>The League was founded later, when life had stabilized enough that people had time to be bored and angry again. The Founders argued that organized combat would absorb the energy that was otherwise getting pointed at, for example, the lobster vendor. The argument worked. We are here now. We have rules. Please read the rule book.</p>
        <p>The Float Foundation can answer follow-up questions about what your specific city did during the Collapse. The Float Foundation is staffed by interns. Be kind to interns. Some of them are knights' descendants and they have stories.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— League Orientation Pamphlet, Eighth Edition</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Origin of the Balloon Contract</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · widely disputed · provenance unclear</div>
        <p>The Balloon Contract was not, originally, about balloons.</p>
        <p>The first League matches were just fistfights. Then someone — usually credited as a Beaver named "Splinter" Tooth, though no record of any Splinter Tooth survives — proposed that fights would be safer if combatants signed an agreement that they had three "lives," and after three knockouts they had to leave the pit. This worked, sort of, except nobody could figure out how to count knockouts in real time during a fast scrum.</p>
        <p>Splinter Tooth (allegedly) suggested that each combatant carry three balloons, and that judges pop one balloon per knockout. The popped balloons were both a visible scoreboard and a physical reminder. Everyone agreed.</p>
        <p>Several decades later, a chemist in Boise developed a stable, weight-tunable lifting gas, which she named Float Gas. The League adopted Float Gas immediately. Now the balloons floated. Now knights felt lighter. Now there was a chemical undercurrent that could be regulated, taxed, and possibly a controlled substance.</p>
        <p>The Float Gas refinery in Boise is now the source of all League-spec balloons. Boise Brawling Badgers get the heaviest gas as a courtesy. The Otters' gas is the lightest, which is why the Otters move so well. The Foxes get desert-formula gas, which expands at high altitude. None of this is a metaphor.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "A People's History of Float," p. 47</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Stadium Wars</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · also called "The Concession War" by people who were there</div>
        <p>Between League Years 7 and 8, six cities tried to buy each other's stadiums. Reno started it. Reno wanted the Brewer's Reach Pitch, because Reno wanted the Reach Beaver mascot to wear a gold visor on a Reno banner. Boston refused. Reno offered a sum of bits that, if it had been deposited, would have caused the Boston league treasurer to retire that afternoon. The bits never moved.</p>
        <p>Other cities watched. Tampa Sharks, smelling money, offered to buy the Boise Float Gas Foundry. Boise refused on the grounds that the Foundry's slag was "still warm." Fresno offered to buy the Helena Glacier Rink and convert it to a citrus orchard. Helena's response is preserved in League Archives and consists, in its entirety, of the word "no" repeated forty-eight times.</p>
        <p>Commissioner Whiskerstone settled the dispute by sitting on every disputed stadium's title deed for one consecutive month. By the end, no city was willing to negotiate further, because no city wanted Whiskerstone to come back. The matter is now a permanent League matter: <em>stadiums belong to their cities, immutably</em>.</p>
        <p>The unintended consequence is that the League now sometimes hosts games in <em>neutral venues</em>. The Reno Hallowtide Hollow, the League Foundation Hall, the Kansas Dust Court — these are all rotating-host arenas that nobody owns. They were built specifically to be unbuyable. They have made the League much better.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— League Office, Stadium Adjudication Committee, official summary</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Year the Foundry Did Not Open</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · widely whispered · officially "no comment"</div>
        <p>In League Year 6, the Boise Float Gas Foundry stopped producing for forty-one days. League officials refused to say why. The Boise Brawling Badgers played that season with reserve gas — old, denser, less reliable. They went 2-12. They have never spoken about it.</p>
        <p>Three weeks into the shutdown, an unsigned letter arrived at the League Office. It was written on plain card stock, in pencil, in a neat round hand. It read: "WE KNOW WHAT YOU PUT IN THE GAS. STOP." The Office did not respond. The shutdown ended six days later. The Foundry resumed production. No one, including the Foundry's senior chemist, has acknowledged the letter exists.</p>
        <p>The Boise Brawling Badgers have, since League Year 6, retained an additional staff member whose only job is to taste a small amount of every batch of Float Gas before it ships. This staff member is paid in cured salmon and is not, as far as anyone has confirmed, an actual badger.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous, Boise local newspaper, archived</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>The Reno Heist</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · disputed · Raccoons say it didn't happen</div>
        <p>The most-scavenged item in League history — Bristlebeard's Riot Shield — was, briefly, in Reno. It was not supposed to be in Reno.</p>
        <p>The Shield was lost on the field in Season 6's final, after its seventh carrier (Wikrai Wartooth Jr., Wolves) fell. League protocol says lost gear is collected by League stewards and returned to a neutral vault overnight. In Season 6's case, the gear was collected, the gear was bagged, the gear was loaded into the steward's truck, and the truck arrived at the vault with the bag intact and the Shield missing.</p>
        <p>The Reno Rascal Raccoons opened the next season with a Tank named Mok Mok wearing a riot shield with seven tally marks scratched on its inner rim. Mok Mok claims the marks are "from training." The League has not pressed the issue. The Beavers have. The Beavers are still pressing the issue.</p>
        <p>Mok Mok's shield was, allegedly, stolen back during a road game in Season 7. Allegedly. Then re-stolen. Then re-stolen. The Shield is, at time of writing, somewhere on the continent. It is being looked for. The lookers are tired.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Lost &amp; Stolen: A Field Guide," vol. III</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Hedgehog Tradition</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · contested by the League Office · widely retold in HED circles</div>
        <p>Helena was the last city to be admitted to the League. Helena was not the last city to <em>fight</em>.</p>
        <p>Hedgehog combat predates HED's League admission by several generations. Backyard ranges, mountain-pass exhibition matches, neighborhood three-on-three brawls — the Hedgehogs were running their own tournament circuit for decades before anyone in the League Office knew it existed. Snipers in particular were a Hedgehog speciality: every Hedgehog kid grew up shooting at painted plate targets at distances most League Snipers would consider unsporting.</p>
        <p>This is why Hester Quillstrike, who joined HED's roster as a rookie in League Year 8, was already a finished sniper when she arrived. She set the 412-pixel record in HED's inaugural season at the Glacier Rink. The League Office initially insisted the shot must have been a measurement error. The League Office sent two officials with calipers. The officials confirmed the measurement. The officials apologized. Hester, who had killed three Beavers in the same match that day, accepted the apology silently.</p>
        <p>Three months later Hester died in a coordinated Otter rush at the same Rink. The Hedgehogs played the rest of the season with her quiver pinned to the dugout wall. The 412-pixel record was not broken, only matched, by Sundip Hootington Jr. before his own death later that season. The mark is now jointly held — posthumously, by both shooters.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Quill &amp; Crossbow: A History of Helena Combat," ch. 7</div>
      </article>

      <article class="history">
        <h3>On the Long Marketing Group</h3>
        <div class="meta">A history · official LMG corporate history · annotated by detractors</div>
        <p>The Long Marketing Group was founded in League Year 4 by a Tampa coastal salvage crew that had recovered, intact, a single working broadcast tower. The tower had survived the Collapse only because it had been buried under a hurricane wreck the previous year and forgotten about. The salvage crew dug it up, repaired it, and discovered they could broadcast.</p>
        <p>What they decided to broadcast was the League. The League at this point was a regional curiosity — Boston, Wichita, Tampa, Boise had matches; nobody outside those cities watched. The salvage crew (now incorporated, now legally the LMG) made a proposal to the Commissioner: exclusive broadcast rights, in exchange for promoting matches to anyone with a working antenna.</p>
        <p>The Commissioner, who was new in office, agreed. The contract she signed in LY 4 is still in force. The LMG has interpreted "exclusive" expansively. They have built additional towers, networked them, and now control which matches air and which don't. Their slogan, "If it's not on LMG, it didn't happen," is corporate self-mythologizing — but it is also, in a real sense, true. Off-air matches are off-record matches.</p>
        <p>The LMG funds half of the Float Foundation. The Float Foundation, in exchange, does not investigate the LMG. The Commissioner has, on at least one occasion, sat on an LMG executive's office chair until they agreed to broadcast a Beavers match. The chair was uncomfortable. The match was broadcast. The Commissioner did not move for fourteen hours.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— LMG Corporate History, year ten · author redacted</div>
      </article>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== STADIUMS ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="stadiums">
      <article class="history">
        <h3>The League's 33 Stadiums</h3>
        <div class="meta">A field guide · maintained by the Float Foundation Atlas Committee · live previews</div>
        <p>Every match is played at one of the venues below. Stadium assignment is rotational across the season — your league's bracket draws a random venue each match (the same seed always picks the same stadium). Cards are grouped by host city; neutral venues appear at the end. Each preview is rendered live from the same biome data the simulator uses.</p>
      </article>
      <div class="lore-stadium-section">
        <h4>Boston</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="grass" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Brewer's Reach Pitch</h3>
            <div class="meta">BVR home · open-air · grass turf · no ambient · capacity ca. 18,000</div>
            <p>A working-class football pitch with mowing stripes still visible across the salt-cured grass. Small dandelions cluster near the corners; the pebbles around the perimeter were pulled from the Reach itself by Beavers in the league's first off-season. Gold field lines, modest center circle, no theatrical lighting — this is the league's oldest playing surface and it doesn't need to dress up.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The original venue. Match #1 was played here in League Year 1, when the only opponents were two scrimmage squads from the same Beaver engineering guild. The lobster vendor at Concession Row pre-dates even that — she was already there before the inaugural match, and remains. Her booth has not moved. Some claim it cannot be moved.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="bostonBay" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Boston Brackish Bay</h3>
            <div class="meta">BVR (Otters often play here away) · outdoor tide pool · grass + seaweed mix · bubble ambient · capacity ca. 6,000</div>
            <p>Tide pools at low water along the rocky coast. Seaweed strands sway in the shallow basins; clumps of grass tuft cluster on the rocks above the waterline; a few pieces of orange-pink coral rest in the deeper pools. Bubbles rise occasionally from the silt. Cool teal floor, soft accent lighting.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Beavers built it as a low-tide-only practice annex; the Olympia Otters petitioned for visiting privileges and were granted them in LY 6. The Otters now schedule one home-style 'away' game here per season. The Sharks find the tide-pool surface slippery and have lost there twice.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Phoenix</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="desert" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Burning Sands Bowl</h3>
            <div class="meta">FOX home · open-air · packed sand floor · heat-shimmer ambient · capacity ca. 24,000</div>
            <p>Half-buried in dust-bowl sand, with sandstone tufts and three or four resilient cacti scattered across the field. Pebbles half-bake into the floor. The vignette is warm orange — the sun is always somewhere over the western corner. White accent lines have been re-painted four times this season.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Foxes' home field, built into a natural depression at the edge of post-Collapse Phoenix. The bowl shape gives the crowd a steep-walled coliseum feel. The Foxes' raider-crew origins are in the gravel and the sand here; their first formal League match was a duel-by-challenge against the Owls and the Foxes won.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="phoenixSmoke" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Phoenix Smoke Stack</h3>
            <div class="meta">FOX (sometimes hosts BDG) · outdoor smelting yard · sand + crack + ember mix · ember ambient · capacity ca. 11,000</div>
            <p>Sand floor with deep cracks running through, embers smoldering in the cracks, the occasional cactus tough enough to survive the heat. The air is hot and the vignette is darker than the Bowl — the sky is reddish, not orange.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built on top of an old smelting yard the Foxes leased from a Boise consortium. The Phoenix Foxes host the Boise Brawling Badgers here once a season as a courtesy game; the Badgers historically lose. The crack lines along the floor are real and not decorative, though League safety officials have asked them not to be advertised that way.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Seattle</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="nightwoods" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Seattle Sleepless Forest</h3>
            <div class="meta">OWL home · outdoor · forested · firefly ambient · capacity ca. 14,500</div>
            <p>Low light. Dark teal floor, scattered grass tufts, dark pebbles. Bioluminescent mushrooms glow softly in clusters at the field edges; fireflies drift overhead in pulsing dots. The accent lighting is cool blue, almost purple, and the vignette is dark enough to feel like genuine night.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Owls won't play during the day if they don't have to. The Sleepless Forest stadium is built into actual old-growth pacific forest, with the field cut from a clearing. Power for the field lights runs off the same century-old hydro line that lights the rest of the Seattle district. The Owls' nocturnal pit-fight origins are baked into the venue's everything.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="badminton" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Seattle Soft Net Hall</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · weatherproof · indoor · wood floor · no ambient · capacity ca. 4,000</div>
            <p>A small indoor venue. Wood floor with subtle painted lines for badminton singles and doubles play, the white markings clean and fresh. A scattering of pebbles along the perimeter (where grounds crew haven't fully cleaned). Soft warm-white lighting from above; very modest decoration.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built by the Owls because Seattle's weather makes the Sleepless Forest unplayable for two months a year. Officially a 'neutral venue' so other teams can use it during those months without paying rent. The badminton court markings are kept because the Owls play recreational badminton in the off-season and consider them sacred.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Tampa</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="volleyball" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Tampa Tornado Sands</h3>
            <div class="meta">SHK home · outdoor beach · sand floor · heat-shimmer ambient · capacity ca. 16,000</div>
            <p>Yellow-tan sand with the volleyball court's net dotted down the middle and attack lines marked in faint white. Sand tufts cluster across the field. The vignette has a coastal blue tint at the corners.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built on the literal site where Tampa's previous indoor arena was destroyed by an actual tornado in Season 3. The Sharks refused to rebuild indoors. The Sands has hosted every Tampa home match since, in all weather. The sand is replenished annually from a coastal salvage operation the Sharks themselves run.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="city" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Tampa Cross Street</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · closed-off intersection · outdoor city · asphalt floor · no ambient · capacity ca. 5,500</div>
            <p>Dark asphalt with a yellow-painted center line, a few manhole covers (functional!), oil stains, and a regrettable amount of gum. Pebbles cluster near the edges. No ambient particles — this is meant to feel like a real city street.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A literal city street the League closes to traffic for matches. Originally adopted because the Sharks needed a venue to host the Phoenix Foxes during a Tampa rebuilding period (Phoenix preferred road-style fights). It's so popular it stuck. Local residents along the street are paid a small bit-stipend per match for the noise.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="tampaTideside" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Tampa Tideside Court</h3>
            <div class="meta">SHK · outdoor secondary · outdoor beach · sand + basketball court · heat-shimmer ambient · capacity ca. 7,000</div>
            <p>A basketball court drawn directly on the beach. White lines crisp despite the sand drifting across them. The court markings include a center circle and three-point arcs; sand tufts cluster against the boundary lines. Warm coastal lighting.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Sharks play their inter-season scrimmages here — half-court basketball, half-arena combat. The Pawington Tradition (asking corpses' permission before scavenging) was first observed by visiting Kits players on this court in LY 6. The Sharks were charmed. The Sharks did not adopt it.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Portland</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="jungle" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Portland Greenhouse</h3>
            <div class="meta">PND home · indoor · jungle floor · falling leaf ambient · capacity ca. 12,000</div>
            <p>Dark green floor with grass tufts, decorative leaves, and a few hanging vines around the edges. The vignette is a deep tropical-green; the lighting is warm and humid-looking. Leaves drift down from the upper unseen canopy at irregular intervals.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Ironically named: the Pandas are the slowest-moving team in the League, and 'greenhouse' suggests something that grows. The dome is real — a glass-and-steel structure repurposed from a pre-Collapse botanical garden. The Pandas sleep here between matches. Sometimes they forget which match is next.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="portlandCinder" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Portland Cinder Park</h3>
            <div class="meta">PND home (autumn rotation) · outdoor · scorched grass + leaves · ember ambient · capacity ca. 9,500</div>
            <p>Burnt-orange leaves scattered over volcanic-scorched ground. Embers smolder in the cracks. A few stubborn grass tufts. The lighting is warm and low; it's always evening here.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A field that burned during the Collapse and was never replanted. The Pandas held a pre-season scrimmage on it once and decided they liked the texture. Now Portland's autumn matches run here. The cinder underfoot is real and gets warm during long matches; players are issued reflective insoles.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Reno</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="neon" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Reno Neon Strip Coliseum</h3>
            <div class="meta">RCC home · indoor · synthwave floor · neon spark ambient · capacity ca. 22,000</div>
            <p>Very dark purple-black floor with a magenta-and-cyan grid pattern of glowing dots. Neon stripes run along the perimeter. Sparks rise from the field — bright streaks in magenta or cyan — and the vignette is a deep neon-cherry purple.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built into a former casino strip. The neon signs that used to advertise gambling now power the field lighting. Reno is the only city in the league whose stadium grid still runs on its original pre-Collapse circuitry — which is also why the Raccoons sometimes get away with stealing things during matches: half the surveillance equipment is on the same fritzy power line.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="retro80s" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Reno Retrowave Plaza</h3>
            <div class="meta">RCC secondary · outdoor plaza · synthwave floor · no ambient · capacity ca. 8,000</div>
            <p>Hot pink and purple gradient floor with grid dots and glowing stripes. Less aggressive than the Coliseum, more public-square in feel. The vignette is purple-magenta; no airborne particles, just the field lighting.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Raccoons' off-night venue. Locals who can't afford Coliseum tickets watch matches here on a folding-chair basis. The Plaza was originally a parking lot painted by an artist who refused to be credited; the artist is widely believed to have been the Commissioner before her League appointment, on a sabbatical year.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="tennis" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Reno Open Court</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · tennis surface · outdoor · clay court · no ambient · capacity ca. 7,000</div>
            <p>Clay-orange floor with crisp white singles, doubles, and service lines. A few sand tufts and small pebbles in the corners. The lighting is warm-amber.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built for tennis tournaments the league hosted briefly before realizing tennis is a worse spectator sport than dueling balloon-knights. The clay surface is now a regular fight venue — slick, slidey, and surprising. Sundip Hootington Jr. fired the longest kill shot in League history (412 pixels) here, in LY 8.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="halloween" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Reno Hallowtide Hollow</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · seasonal · outdoor graveyard · pumpkin + grave + grass · bat ambient · capacity ca. 11,000</div>
            <p>Dark purple-black floor with grass tufts, jack-o'-lantern pumpkins, and small standing tombstones scattered across. Bats fly in silhouette across the upper field, wings flapping. The vignette is the deepest purple in the league.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Hallowtide host venue every year. Built specifically to be unbuyable during the Stadium Wars. The 'graveyard' aesthetic is purely decorative — none of the tombstones are real, though one is suspected of being someone's actual cousin's. The Wolves famously play their best games here, for reasons no Wolf has agreed to discuss.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="renoNeonReef" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Reno Neon Reef</h3>
            <div class="meta">RCC tertiary · indoor aquarium · neon + seaweed + coral · bubble ambient · capacity ca. 5,500</div>
            <p>Deep blue floor with magenta-and-cyan grid dots, swaying seaweed, and bright pink-orange coral fragments. Bubbles drift upward; neon stripes pulse along the edges. The vignette is rich indigo-purple.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A defunct aquarium retrofitted by Raccoon engineers. The original tanks were drained but the lighting kept; the Raccoons added the neon grid pattern themselves. Some fish murals remain on the perimeter walls. Players occasionally complain of phantom-ammonia smell, which the Raccoons say does not exist.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Fresno</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="blossom" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Fresno Petalfield</h3>
            <div class="meta">FRG home · outdoor garden · grass + petals · falling petal ambient · capacity ca. 13,000</div>
            <p>Soft green floor with cherry-blossom pink petals scattered everywhere. Grass tufts in clusters, a few pebbles. The vignette is a dusky lavender. Petals continuously drift down across the field.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built in an abandoned cherry orchard the Frogs leased after the Collapse. The trees are still alive at the perimeter — they bloom on their own schedule, and matches here are postponed if the bloom isn't aligned with the broadcast window. The Frogs' speedster style has been compared to the petals' drift; the Frogs find this comparison condescending.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="fresnoSpringCourt" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Fresno Spring Court</h3>
            <div class="meta">FRG secondary · outdoor meadow · grass + flowers + tennis lines · falling petal ambient · capacity ca. 6,500</div>
            <p>A tennis court drawn over a flowering spring meadow. Pink and yellow flowers scatter through the grass; dandelions cluster at the corners. Crisp white tennis lines cross the green. Petals continuously drift down.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Hosts the league's annual Pollen Open mid-spring, in which the Frogs play exhibition matches against any other team brave enough to court the Frogs on a tennis surface. The Frogs are 5-1 lifetime in Pollen Open matches. The 1 loss was to the Otters, who refused to comment.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Helena</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="ice" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Helena Glacier Rink</h3>
            <div class="meta">HED home · indoor · ice surface · falling snow ambient · capacity ca. 9,500</div>
            <p>A pale blue-white ice rink with cracks visible through the surface. Snow tufts and dark pebbles cluster around the edges; the rink lines are very white against the cool floor. Snow drifts down from above. The vignette is steely-blue.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Helena's home field, technically a frozen-over lake at the foot of the mountains. The newest Hedgehog stadium (built LY 8 alongside the team's formal admission to the League). Hester Quillstrike set the original 412-pixel kill record here in HED's inaugural season, before falling to an Otter rush. The rink ice is genuine — Float Gas balloons handle cold exceptionally well.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="helenaHigh" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Helena High Garden</h3>
            <div class="meta">HED secondary · outdoor mountain garden · snow + petals + evergreens · petal ambient · capacity ca. 4,500</div>
            <p>Snowy floor with pink cherry petals scattered across, and a few small evergreen trees standing at the edges. Pebbles in clusters. Petals drift down despite the cold. The vignette is a soft mountain-blue.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A monastery garden the Hedgehogs adopted as a venue. The cherry trees were planted by a pre-Collapse caretaker who genetically modified them to bloom in subzero temperatures. The Hedgehogs claim spiritual significance here. The Hedgehogs play three matches per season at the High Garden, and have not lost one in three years.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="helenaStone" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Helena Stone Garden</h3>
            <div class="meta">HED tertiary · indoor cave · stalagmites + leaves · drifting leaf ambient · capacity ca. 3,000</div>
            <p>A cave floor with dark stone walls. Stalagmites point up at the field edges; bright crystals catch the light in clusters; russet leaves cover much of the floor (blown in from the slopes outside). The vignette is the darkest charcoal-brown.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A natural cave at the foot of the Helena range, used by the Hedgehogs for cold-weather practice. The stalagmites are real and not decorative; the leaves blow in through a crack in the upper east wall. Hedgehogs love this venue: low ceilings give cover, and the Hedgehogs are short enough to navigate them. Visiting teams find it claustrophobic.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Kansas</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="basketball" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Kansas Hardwood Hall</h3>
            <div class="meta">KIT home · indoor · wood court · no ambient · capacity ca. 12,000</div>
            <p>Wood-orange floor with sharp white basketball court markings: three-point arcs, key rectangles, free-throw circles, the center jump-ball ring. A handful of pebbles near the perimeter. Warm-amber lighting; no airborne particles.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Kits' home field — a converted basketball arena. The team's signature acrobatic vertical-mobility style was developed here, where the line markings become reference points for parkour-like positioning. The Kits practice court-marker-to-court-marker drills here daily. They've won zero championships. They are loved anyway.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="kansasDust" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Kansas Dust Court</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · prairie · outdoor · sand-dusted court · heat-shimmer ambient · capacity ca. 5,000</div>
            <p>An outdoor basketball court covered in prairie sand. White court markings still show through. Sand tufts and small pebbles drift across the lines; the vignette is a warm dust-orange.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A prairie venue several hours west of Kansas City. The Kits practice here when the Hardwood Hall is too hot indoors during summer. The dust is not decorative. Visitors complain. Visiting teams have, several times, requested 'cleaning' before matches; the Kits have, several times, declined.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Wichita</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="cave" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Wichita Underdig</h3>
            <div class="meta">WLF home · underground · cave floor · drip ambient · capacity ca. 4,500</div>
            <p>Dark stone floor with stalagmites, glowing blue crystals, and dark pebbles. Tiny water drops fall from somewhere unseen above. The lighting is dim, the vignette deep charcoal.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The Wolves' practice cave network, repurposed as a home venue. Pack tactics work especially well here — sight lines are constrained, every corridor encourages coordinated multi-knight engagement. The bow ritual was first performed at the Underdig (formally; informally it had been done elsewhere). The Wolves consider this venue spiritually important and play here only at major matches.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="autumn" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Wichita Wheat Belt</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · between cities · outdoor prairie · russet leaves + grass · drifting leaf ambient · capacity ca. 6,000</div>
            <p>Russet-brown floor with grass tufts and many fallen orange leaves. Pebbles scattered. The lighting is warm gold; the vignette is autumn-amber.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built between Wichita and Kansas City as a neutral prairie venue. Hosts inter-team matches that need a 'soft' surface — the Wheat Belt's leaves cushion falls without altering combat outcomes. Hester Quillstrike practiced sniper drills on a similar field as a child, before HED was admitted to the League.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="wichitaWitching" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Wichita Witching Wood</h3>
            <div class="meta">WLF secondary · outdoor forest · grass + glow mushrooms + pumpkins · firefly ambient · capacity ca. 3,500</div>
            <p>Dark forest floor with grass tufts, glowing mushrooms, and small jack-o'-lantern pumpkins scattered through. Fireflies drift overhead. The vignette is the deepest blue-black in the league outside Olympia.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Used by the Wolves during Hallowtide for additional matches the Reno Hollow can't fit. The Wolves love it here — a cousin venue to their Underdig with similar sightline geometry, plus the firefly cover advantages a hunting pack. The pumpkins are real and locally grown by a Wichita farm cooperative; they're rotated weekly.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Boise</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="volcanic" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Float Gas Foundry</h3>
            <div class="meta">BDG home · outdoor industrial · slag floor · ember ambient · capacity ca. 19,000</div>
            <p>Dark charcoal floor with bright orange-red embers and crackled lava lines visible. Pebbles cluster near the edges. The vignette is a deep crimson-black; embers float up from the cracks continuously.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built directly on top of the active Float Gas refinery. The slag underfoot is warm because the refinery is still running below. Boise gets the densest balloon-fill formula because they have first dibs on each batch. The 41-day shutdown of LY 6 happened here; nobody talks about it.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="snowywonder" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Boise High Pass</h3>
            <div class="meta">BDG secondary · outdoor mountain · snow + evergreens · falling snow ambient · capacity ca. 5,500</div>
            <p>Pale blue-white snow floor with dark evergreen trees standing at the edges. Snow tufts and pebbles in clusters. Snow falls steadily; the vignette is the cool blue of an alpine afternoon.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A mountaintop venue used for winter exhibition matches. The Badgers are the only team that can be relied on to maintain combat efficiency in heavy snow — their gear is calibrated for it. The High Pass closes for storms during three months a year, but those three months are reliably the best Badger weather.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="boiseFrostForge" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Boise Frost Forge</h3>
            <div class="meta">BDG industrial-secondary · indoor industrial · ice + ember mix · ember ambient · capacity ca. 4,000</div>
            <p>A floor split between cold blue ice cracks and warm-orange embers. Snow tufts and pebbles scatter the surface. The lighting is contradictory: cold-bright on one side, warm-glow on the other.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">A second Foundry building used for the Float Gas refinement step that requires both deep cold AND high heat. Hostile to almost every team except the Badgers, who have been Float-Gas-tolerant since their founding. Sundip Hootington Jr. attempted to play an exhibition match here in LY 7 and finished it on a stretcher. Owls do not return to the Forge.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>Olympia</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="underwater" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Olympia Riverbed</h3>
            <div class="meta">OTR home · underwater · river silt floor · bubble ambient · capacity ca. 7,500</div>
            <p>Deep blue silt floor with sea-green seaweed, bright coral fragments, and small smooth pebbles. Bubbles rise continuously through the field. The vignette is a midnight-blue; the lighting is cool-aquatic.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Yes, the Riverbed is actually underwater. The Otters compete in pressurized helmets; visiting teams compete in pressurized helmets they have to lease from the Otters. The Otters' equipment-scavenging fluency was developed in part because river-bed gear-recovery is non-trivial. The Otters' fluid combat style is more or less indistinguishable from swimming.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="spring" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Olympia Spring Lawn</h3>
            <div class="meta">OTR secondary · outdoor meadow · grass + flowers + dandelions · petal ambient · capacity ca. 4,000</div>
            <p>Bright green meadow with grass tufts, pink and yellow flowers in clusters, and dandelions in the corners. The vignette is a spring-bright periwinkle. Petals drift down softly.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">An above-water practice meadow for Otter knights who need to remember what the surface looks like. Used for spring matches and any away-game with a visiting team that has refused to compete underwater. Olympia's unofficial motto for the Spring Lawn is 'we still don't see why you can't just hold your breath.'</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="olympiaPetalPool" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>Olympia Petal Pool</h3>
            <div class="meta">OTR cherry-bloom · underwater · seaweed + coral + petals · petal ambient · capacity ca. 3,000</div>
            <p>Deep blue floor where Fresno cherry petals float on the surface and seaweed sways below. Pink and orange coral fragments cluster in pools. Petals drift down through the water.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">Built downstream from a Fresno cherry orchard's annual bloom. Petals genuinely drift downstream and accumulate on the Pool's surface. The Otters host the Frogs here once a year — both teams play unusually polite matches at the Petal Pool, citing the 'mood.'</p>
          </div>
        </div>
        <h4>League / Neutral</h4>
        <div class="lore-stadium-card">
          <canvas class="lore-stadium-preview" data-biome-id="holiday" width="220" height="124"></canvas>
          <div>
            <h3>League Foundation Hall</h3>
            <div class="meta">neutral · ceremonial · indoor festive hall · snow + gifts + ornaments · falling snow ambient · capacity ca. 8,000</div>
            <p>Dark green floor with snow tufts, wrapped gifts in red and green, gold-and-cream ornaments, and small evergreen trees clustered along the edges. Soft warm lighting; gentle snowfall throughout.</p>
            <p class="city-lore">The League's official Foundation Day venue. Built specifically to be neutral — not owned by any team — and exists for the annual celebration of the inaugural match (LY 1). The lobster vendor caters Foundation Day; she travels from Boston. Foundation Day is the only date the lobster vendor leaves Brewer's Reach. She returns the following morning.</p>
          </div>
        </div>
      </div>

    <!-- ===== BIOGRAPHIES ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="bios">
      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Commissioner Margaux "Fang" Whiskerstone</h3>
        <div class="meta">Maine Coon · b. uncertain · League Office, head</div>
        <p>The Commissioner has been the Commissioner since League Year 4. Before that, the Commissioner was, in order: a port-authority arbitration officer in Boston, a syndicated columnist in Tampa, the proprietor of a moderately successful tea shop in Olympia, and (briefly) a competitive sleeper. None of this should be relevant. All of it has come up.</p>
        <p>Whiskerstone is famous for her dispute resolution method, which is to physically sit on the contested object until both parties agree to a settlement. This works because the Commissioner is a Maine Coon, and Maine Coons are extraordinarily heavy when they want to be. The Office has documented at least two cases where she sat on a stadium's title deed for the better part of a fortnight before either city blinked.</p>
        <p>In a rare interview, the Commissioner once said: "I do not enforce the rules. I am the rules. Please do not test this." The interviewer's follow-up question is recorded in the League archives only as "[laughter]."</p>
        <p>Her chair is famously raised on a small dais. The League Office insists this is "for visibility." Everyone in the Office knows it is because Maine Coons hate not being the tallest creature in the room.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Who's Who in the League," updated annually</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Old Bristlebeard the First</h3>
        <div class="meta">Beaver · Tank · BVR · in the Hall · died LY 5 R5</div>
        <p>The first martyr. The first true Beaver. The patriarch of a name that has now been retired twice and re-fielded three times.</p>
        <p>Old Bristlebeard the First was, by all accounts, slow. He moved at the speed of an unloaded freight wagon. He was the worst Tank in his rookie cohort by the metric of footwork; he was the best by the metric of "still standing at the end of the round." He led the Beavers in damage absorbed for five consecutive seasons, never won a single match's MVP award, and did not seem to mind.</p>
        <p>He died in the championship final of Season 5, holding a position that did not need to be held. He took three Beavers with him in the blast — a friendly-fire call that was, in his case, a strategic call: he ate the grenade so the Wolves could not use it on his squad. The League has classed it as <em>tactical self-sacrifice</em> and the Wolves still complain about it.</p>
        <p>His Riot Shield is the most-traveled item in League history. His Plasma Mace returned to a Bristlebeard exactly once, for one round, before being lost in the same match.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes plaque, BVR</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Vela Hootington</h3>
        <div class="meta">Owl · Speed · OWL · in the Hall · died LY 8 R7</div>
        <p>The fastest Owl to ever hold a weapon. Vela Hootington's 22-kill streak still stands; the next two knights to break it both died trying.</p>
        <p>Hootington was a paradox. She moved like fog and spoke like she had a clock running. Her teammates loved her and resented her in roughly equal proportion, because she would lap them mid-engagement, and because she would refuse to repeat instructions she'd already given. She was famous for two pieces of equipment: her Plate Cuirass, which she handed to a teammate for a single round (her last) and which has since become known as Vela's Plate Cuirass, and her Stun Baton, which her killer (a Shark named Tomik Splashfang) carried for the rest of his career and then died holding.</p>
        <p>The "Vela's Plate Cuirass" item — the legacy item, in scavenge circulation — is currently believed to be on a knight in Olympia, though that knight has refused to confirm. The teammate she gave it to never spoke about her again. He retired after that season and bought the tea shop the Commissioner used to own. He still works there.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Owl Hall, Sundip Memorial Wing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Mira Pawington, "The Scavenger Queen"</h3>
        <div class="meta">Kit · Speed · KIT · in the Hall · died LY 5 R6</div>
        <p>Mira Pawington holds the league record for scavenges in a career: eleven. She held the record for scavenges in a single match (four) until a Raccoon broke it; she does not particularly mind.</p>
        <p>She specialized in <em>polite</em> scavenging. Pawington was not a thief. She would walk up to a fallen knight, kneel, ask the body's permission (a Kits tradition), and then take the gear. League officials documented this in Season 4, when an opposing knight watching from the bench claimed Pawington had spent a full eight seconds in conversation with a corpse. The bench knight insisted, on record, that the corpse <em>responded</em>. The bench knight was sent home.</p>
        <p>Pawington died with her opponent's Power Cell in her hand, two animation frames from completing the steal. The opponent was a Raccoon. The Raccoon survived the round. The Raccoon kept the Power Cell. The Raccoon did not apologize. The Kits hold a moment of polite silence for Pawington in their pre-season retreat every year, and the Raccoons hold a moment of slightly less polite silence at the same time.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Quiet Hands: A Memoir of Scavenging," posthumous</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Felix Marshmallow</h3>
        <div class="meta">Panda · Tank · PND · in the Hall · grenade-safety drill namesake · died LY 7 R5</div>
        <p>Felix Marshmallow was the kind of teammate every team wants. He brought baked goods to practice. He learned everyone's siblings' names. He was famous for being the slowest Panda on a roster of slow Pandas, and proud of it.</p>
        <p>He died in Season 7, Round 5, of a fragmentation grenade thrown by a teammate from approximately seven feet away. The teammate had been aiming at an Otter. The Otter was, in fairness, on the other side of Felix Marshmallow.</p>
        <p>The League's grenade safety drills are now mandatory in pre-season for all teams. They were not mandatory before Season 7. The drills are, on the books, called the "Marshmallow Protocol." On the field they are called "Felix Drills." The teammate who threw the grenade has refused to grenade since. He has refused to grenade so emphatically that the Pandas now have a roster carve-out for him: he is <em>not allowed</em> to handle explosives, and his contract specifies it.</p>
        <p>Felix Marshmallow's family receives a benefit from the Float Foundation. The benefit is paid in lobster, at his family's request, sourced from Boston.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Pandas Roster Memorial, Portland Greenhouse</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>"Splinter" Tooth (allegedly)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Beaver · disputed historical figure · "the Balloon's Father"</div>
        <p>Did Splinter Tooth exist? No one alive can confirm.</p>
        <p>League folklore credits Splinter Tooth with proposing the Balloon Contract in the League's first year — three balloons, three lives, popped publicly so the scoreboard could be read by the audience. The story is repeated by every Beaver coach to every Beaver rookie. The story has minor variations. In some versions Splinter Tooth was a champion who later retired to the lobster trade. In others, Splinter Tooth was a drunk who proposed the system on a bet and then forgot. In one version, recorded by a Reno historian who is not trusted, Splinter Tooth was a <em>Wolf</em> who infiltrated the Beavers' first roster.</p>
        <p>No League roster from Year 1 lists a "Splinter Tooth." This is taken, by Beavers, as proof of how good Splinter was. It is taken, by everyone else, as proof that Splinter Tooth never existed. Both interpretations agree that the Balloon Contract is real, valid, and binding.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— "Founding Myths," League Library Reading Room</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Hester Quillstrike</h3>
        <div class="meta">Hedgehog · Sniper · HED · in the Hall · died LY 8 R3 · 412-pixel record co-holder</div>
        <p>Hester Quillstrike was a finished sniper before her League career began. She grew up shooting at painted plate targets in the foothills of Helena, where Hedgehog kids learn ranged combat the way Boston kids learn to use a hammer — quietly, daily, without ceremony. By the time HED was admitted to the League in LY 8, Hester was already, in any meaningful sense, the best sniper alive.</p>
        <p>She debuted in HED's inaugural season at the Helena Glacier Rink. Her first League kill was a 290-pixel shot through a Wolf's helmet visor in Round 2 of HED's opening match. Three matches later, in HED's third match, she made a 412-pixel rail shot across the full diagonal of the Rink, into a fleeing Otter Speedster. The League officials sent calipers to verify the distance. They confirmed. They apologized for asking. Hester said nothing.</p>
        <p>In Round 3 of that same match — the match where she set the record — she was killed by a coordinated four-Otter rush who had clearly identified her as the threat. She fell at her sniper position, her quiver still half-full, her Railgun Rifle still in her hand. The Rifle was scavenged by a Sundip Hootington Jr. shortly afterward. Sundip went on to match her record exactly, posthumously, before his own death at the Reno Open Court later in LY 8.</p>
        <p>The 412-pixel mark is now co-held by Hester and Sundip. Neither one is alive to claim it. The Hedgehogs consider this fitting.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Helena annex</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Tomik Splashfang the Bold</h3>
        <div class="meta">Shark · Brawl · SHK · in the Hall · died LY 3 R2 · grenade-fumble pioneer</div>
        <p>Tomik Splashfang was the kind of Brawler the league used to have — heavy, loud, charging, and immune to any form of strategic thinking that lasted longer than four seconds. He was the captain of the Sharks' inaugural roster (SHK was founded LY 3) and held that role until his death later that same year.</p>
        <p>His final match was Round 2 of SHK's third game ever. The Sharks were up 2-0 in rounds against the Beavers. Tomik had picked up a Frag Grenade — possibly his own, possibly Bristlebeard the First's, accounts differ — and was preparing to throw it at the Beavers' back zone. He was, at the time, two seconds into a stun applied by a Beaver Sniper. The stun ran out exactly when his throw arm would have completed.</p>
        <p>It did not complete. The grenade exploded in his hand. He took two Beavers with him; he also took himself. The Sharks, in fairness, won that match anyway, but they retired Tomik's number for the season and required all Brawlers to undergo a one-week stun-recovery course before handling explosives. The Marshmallow Protocol of LY 7 cites the Splashfang Incident in its preamble.</p>
        <p>Tomik's Stun Baton entered circulation after his death and was carried by, in order, Brask Ironclaw III and Vela Hootington. Vela died holding it. The Stun Baton is now believed to be in private retirement, somewhere on the Boise mountain pass.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Tampa wing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Crash Wartooth</h3>
        <div class="meta">Wolf · Brawl · WLF · in the Hall · died LY 6 R3 · Crash Rule namesake</div>
        <p>Crash Wartooth was, by Wolf standards, average. By every other team's standards he was terrifying. The Wolves are a hard grading curve.</p>
        <p>He played five seasons as the Wolves' lead Brawler, accruing 41 kills and an 11-kill streak. His pack-coordination instinct was excellent — the Wolves under his roster had a 70% match-win rate during his tenure. He was not personally famous; he wasn't a face-of-the-team kind of knight. He was reliable in the way load-bearing beams are reliable, and the Wolves liked him for it.</p>
        <p>He died at the Brewer's Reach Pitch in Round 3 of a Wolves vs. Beavers match in LY 6. The Beavers had brought three Stun Batons. Crash, doing what Brawlers do, charged. The Beavers stunned him. Crash stayed standing through the first stun (Wolves are tough). The Beavers stunned him again. Crash stayed standing through the second. The Beavers stunned him a third time. Crash, by this point, was unable to defend himself. A Beaver Brawler closed the kill. The full sequence, including all three stuns, took twelve seconds.</p>
        <p>The Wolves spent the next month re-engineering their Brawler kit. The "Crash Rule" — heavy plate is mandatory for any Wolves Brawler — was adopted in pre-season of LY 7 and has been honored ever since. No Wolf Brawler has died to a stun-volley since the Rule was adopted. Three Stun Batons have been thrown at Wolf Brawlers and bounced off without effect; the Beavers consider this insulting and the Wolves consider it correct.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Wichita wing</div>
      </article>

      <article class="bio">
        <h3>Sundip Hootington Jr.</h3>
        <div class="meta">Owl · Sniper · OWL · in the Hall · died LY 8 R8 · 412-pixel record co-holder</div>
        <p>Sundip Hootington Jr. was the son of Sundip Hootington Sr., who was a journeyman Owl Brawler who died unspectacularly in his fourth season. The Junior was, by the family's accounting, supposed to be a Brawler too. He was not. He was a Sniper. The family did not approve until he tied the league's longest-shot record posthumously, by which time the family was no longer in a position to disapprove.</p>
        <p>His career was sparse and excellent. 36 kills across 4 seasons, 13-kill streak, 4 scavenges. He was famously methodical, disliked by his own teammates for refusing to call shots in advance, and feared by Healers for the obvious reason. He took his job seriously enough to make weekly visits to the Hall of Fallen Heroes' Hester Quillstrike memorial — he had carried her Railgun Rifle for two seasons after her death and considered it a debt.</p>
        <p>His final match was at the Reno Open Court in LY 8. He fired a 412-pixel rail shot across the diagonal of the tennis court — exactly tying Hester's record — into a fleeing Frog Speedster. He climbed the Power Core to celebrate. The Power Core, which had been quiet for eight seasons, picked that moment to overload. Sundip did not survive the celebration. The League patched the overload that off-season.</p>
        <p>The Frog Speedster, in fairness, also died of the overload. The Frogs were given partial credit for posthumously denying Sundip his celebration. Hester's Rifle, which Sundip had been carrying, fell at the base of the Power Core and was scavenged in the post-match cleanup by a vault steward who has since requested unpaid leave.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Hall of Fallen Heroes, Sundip Memorial Wing, Seattle</div>
      </article>
    </div>

    <!-- ===== DIARIES ===== -->
    <div class="lore-section" data-loresection="diaries">
      <article class="diary">
        <h3>Diary of a Healer (HED)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Anonymized · seized from a locker room · published with permission</div>
        <p><em>Day one.</em> They told me a Healer's job is to heal. The other rookies laughed when I asked who I should heal. The drill instructor said: "the lowest-HP teammate." I asked: "what if there are two." He said: "pick one." I asked: "how do I pick." He said: "you'll figure it out." I have not figured it out.</p>
        <p><em>Day forty.</em> I have been on the field for six matches now. I follow whoever needs healing. I do not shoot at anyone. The other team's Sniper shoots at me, every time, immediately, before they shoot at anyone else. The drill instructor says this is "expected behavior" and I should "keep my heal target between me and the Sniper." I asked if my heal target knew about this arrangement. He did not answer.</p>
        <p><em>Day eighty.</em> I have learned to wander a little. When my heal target gets too far ahead, or when they turn a corner I cannot follow into, I sometimes drift. The other Healers tell me this is normal. The other Healers all have a thing they think about while drifting. I think about lobster. I have never had lobster. The Beavers' Healer told me about it. I would like to try lobster. The League sells lobster at the Boston concession.</p>
        <p><em>Day one hundred and twelve.</em> I am writing this from the Hall of Fallen Heroes intake desk. They are processing my paperwork. The intake officer told me my matches will be replayed for memorial purposes. She told me I can request that my final match not be one of them. I told her I do not have a preference. I told her I would like a portion of my pension to be sent to the Boston concession in lobster. She wrote it down and underlined it.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous, HED Healer cohort, posthumous</div>
      </article>

      <article class="diary">
        <h3>Diary of a Sniper (SHK)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Tampa Tornado Sharks scout journal · maintained for three seasons</div>
        <p><em>Entry 14.</em> The Healer is the kill. Always. Even if the Healer is not the closest target. Even if the Healer is moving away. The Healer is the kill, because the Healer is the difference between the other team having a third round and the other team going home tonight.</p>
        <p><em>Entry 22.</em> I shot a Healer today. The Healer's heal target took the next four hits in eight seconds and went down on the fifth. I did not need to fire those four hits. The Healer's heal target was already losing the engagement; I just sped it up. The League's notes called it a "tactical erasure." I cannot stop thinking about the word <em>erasure</em>. I do not like it. The notes do not have to like it. The notes only have to be accurate.</p>
        <p><em>Entry 28.</em> The Healers have started wearing red ribbons. The Snipers have, in response, started ignoring the red ribbons. The Healers have, in response to that, started wearing the same colors as their teammates. The Snipers, in response to that, are still shooting them, because the Healer is the kill.</p>
        <p><em>Entry 34.</em> The opposing team's Healer was, today, my old neighbor's daughter. I did not know this at the time. I know now. I do not know if I would have done anything differently. I think the answer is no. The Healer is the kill.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Tampa Sharks scouts' shared journal, Season 7 entries</div>
      </article>

      <article class="diary">
        <h3>Diary of a Grenadier (PND, the Marshmallow Drill)</h3>
        <div class="meta">"To be opened in case of post-traumatic frag review"</div>
        <p>I have not thrown a grenade since Season 7. I will not throw a grenade. I will not handle a grenade. I will not look at a grenade. I will not <em>think</em> about a grenade.</p>
        <p>The League says I am not under disciplinary review. The League says it was an accident. The League sent the report. The League sent flowers. The League sent the Marshmallow Protocol training video, which is fifteen minutes of footage of <em>me</em>, slowed down, with arrows added by an animator, and which I have not watched and will not watch.</p>
        <p>I throw stones now. We do not have a stone-throwing position on the roster, but the coach lets me do it in practice and then quietly takes the stones away before matches. I have a melee bat for matches. The melee bat is fine. The melee bat is not a grenade.</p>
        <p>Felix's family sent me a card last winter. It said "we know it wasn't your fault." I do know it wasn't my fault. I know exactly whose fault it was. The Otter moved. Felix did not. I did everything right except for being three feet too short.</p>
        <p>I am writing this because the league therapist said I should. She did not say I had to mail it.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— anonymous, PND Grenadier, "do not file"</div>
      </article>

      <article class="diary">
        <h3>The Steward's Notebook (League Office, Vault Wing)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Daily log · season 6 · partial</div>
        <p><em>Day 1 of vault rotation.</em> Picked up four armor pieces and one shield. Inventoried. Bagged. Sealed. Loaded. Ten-minute drive to the vault. All five items present on arrival. Vault staff signed.</p>
        <p><em>Day 7.</em> Picked up six items. Inventoried. Bagged. Sealed. Loaded. Ten-minute drive. <em>Five</em> items on arrival. The shield is not in the bag. The bag is sealed. The seal is intact. There is no rip. There is no opening. The shield is, however, not in the bag.</p>
        <p><em>Day 8.</em> Reviewed the truck. The truck is empty. The shield is not in the truck. The shield was, the steward confirms, in the bag at pickup. The shield is, the vault confirms, not in the bag at delivery. The seal is the same seal. We have no theory.</p>
        <p><em>Day 11.</em> The League Office is reviewing the matter. I have been told to keep doing my job. I have been told to keep doing my job <em>without checking the bag mid-route</em>. I have not been told why I should not check the bag.</p>
        <p><em>Day 14.</em> I checked the bag mid-route. The shield was in the bag. I drove the rest of the route. I unsealed the bag in front of the vault staff. The shield was not in the bag. I then unloaded every other item from the truck. The shield was, with apologies, in <em>my own coat pocket</em>. I do not remember putting it there. I have requested unpaid leave.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Steward's Notebook, redacted, vault wing archive</div>
      </article>

      <article class="diary">
        <h3>Diary of a Tank (BDG)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Personal · taster's logbook · Boise Foundry, refinery shift 2</div>
        <p><em>Day 1.</em> Promoted off the field. The leg won't fully extend after Lhor's shot in S5, and the doctors say one more season as a Tank and the leg will not extend at all. The Foundry offered me the taster job. I took it. The Float Gas formula needs a Badger nose, they say. I am a Badger. I have a nose. We will see.</p>
        <p><em>Day 6.</em> The training is, "smell each batch, mark it on a scale of one to seven, and never lie." The senior taster (her name is Krela, she is older than the Foundry) tells me that the scale used to go to ten but they removed eight, nine, and ten because they smelled like things she did not want to remember. I asked what they smelled like. Krela did not answer. I have not asked again.</p>
        <p><em>Day 19.</em> The current batch smells like a six. Krela smells it and says four. We argue. The Foundry chemist comes in and smells it and says five. We compromise on five. The batch goes to the Foxes. The Foxes lose at the Bowl that weekend. I do not know if the loss is the gas's fault. I do not feel it is mine.</p>
        <p><em>Day 41.</em> Foundry shut down today. No reason given. We are paid in full. The senior taster tells me to "go home and not check the gas in your apartment." I check the gas in my apartment. The gas in my apartment is fine. The gas in my apartment is from a batch I rated a seven. I have a moment of pride.</p>
        <p><em>Day 41+1.</em> An unsigned letter arrived at the Office today. I am told not to discuss the letter. I am told the Foundry will reopen in approximately six weeks. I am told to keep doing my job during that time. I am being paid to not work. This is, I am told, "the reserve gas period." I am told the reserve gas is lighter and burns clean. I am told it is fine. I do not believe it is fine. I am instructed to believe it is fine.</p>
        <p><em>Day 87.</em> Foundry reopens. The gas chemist apologizes to no one in particular. I rate the new batch a six. Krela rates it a six. The chemist rates it a six. We agree. The batch goes to the Wolves. The Wolves win. I no longer believe the gas affects the matches. I believe Krela has been lying for thirty years about the scale only going to seven.</p>
        <p><em>Day 88.</em> Krela retired. She gave me her booklet of ratings. The booklet has entries on a scale of one to ten. I have not opened the back half of the booklet.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Taster's Logbook, Foundry Annex, partial transcription</div>
      </article>
    </div>

        <h3>Match Log: Season 6 Final, BVR vs WLF (Boston, Brewer's Reach)</h3>

        <p>22:18 — Wartooth counters with Stun Baton. BST stunned, drops to one balloon.</p>
        <p>22:22 — Wartooth's three-Wolf rotation closes from the south side. Field is now five-vs-three in WLF's favor.</p>
        <p>22:31 — BST attempts to retrieve Plasma Mace from a fallen Beaver Brawler. BST stunned again. WLF rotation closes the kill.</p>
        <p>22:34 — Bristlebeard the Second falls. The Beavers begin a slow-motion retreat to the back zone.</p>
        <p>22:48 — A single Beaver Healer reaches the back zone. The Healer is alone.</p>
        <p>22:52 — The Wolves begin their classic pack engagement. Five knights converge on the lone Healer.</p>
        <p>22:54 — The Beaver Healer drops a Health Pack at her own feet. She heals to full balloons. The Wolves arrive.</p>
        <p>22:55 — The Healer pops her own Power Cell. The Healer enters berserk mode. The Wolves engage anyway.</p>
        <p>23:11 — The Healer is the last Beaver standing. The Wolves are at three knights. The crowd is on its feet. This is the longest "last knight" sequence in BVR history.</p>
        <p>23:34 — The Healer falls to a Wolf Sniper firing from the back of the stadium. The Wolves win.</p>
        <p>23:36 — The Wolves do not celebrate. They walk to the Healer's body. They form a circle. The Wolf captain bows. The crowd is silent. This will later be called "The Bow."</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Boston Sportscaster Booth, Season 6 final</div>

      <article class="log">
        <h3>Match Log: Season 8 QF3, OWL vs FRG (Reno Open Court)</h3>
        <div class="meta">Note: this match was played on a tennis court, which neither team practices on</div>
        <p>00:34 — Both teams enter the court. Neither team is sure where the spawn lines are.</p>
        <p>00:48 — A Frog speedster slides on the clay surface and rolls into the net. The Frog comes up with the net wrapped around the Frog's head. The match resumes anyway.</p>
        <p>01:12 — Sundip Hootington Jr. (Owl Sniper) sets up at the back service line. Sundip is on his last career month. Sundip does not know it.</p>
        <p>02:48 — A Frog Grenadier throws a frag grenade. The grenade lands on the singles sideline. The grenade rolls along the sideline for nineteen feet without losing pace, hits a Frog Healer, and detonates.</p>
        <p>02:52 — The Frog Healer is, against expectation, fine. The Frog Healer's heal beam, on the other hand, has been knocked into a horizontal arc that points only at the south wall. The Frog Healer cannot heal her teammates for the rest of the match.</p>
        <p>04:03 — Power Core spawns at center court. Both teams ignore it.</p>
        <p>04:31 — Power Core is finally captured by an Owl Brawler who needed a place to lean.</p>
        <p>06:14 — Sundip Hootington Jr. is, by this point, the only Owl on the field. He has 9 kills. The Frogs have one survivor: a Speedster.</p>
        <p>06:22 — The Frog Speedster runs the clay. Sundip tracks. The clay is slick. The Speedster is not slick. The Speedster falls.</p>
        <p>06:23 — Sundip fires the kill shot. He is on the back service line. The shot is a 412-pixel railshot. It is the longest kill shot in League history. It will stand for three full seasons.</p>
        <p>06:24 — Sundip walks to the Power Core to celebrate. Sundip <em>climbs onto</em> the Power Core to celebrate. Sundip's celebration is interrupted by the Power Core's overload sequence, which has not been seen in eight seasons. Sundip does not survive the celebration.</p>
        <p>06:24 — The match ends in an Owl win. The match ends in Sundip Hootington Jr.'s permadeath. Both things are true. The crowd is unsure how to react. The Frog Speedster, in fairness, also dies of the overload, which gives the Frogs partial credit on the post-mortem stats.</p>
        <div class="signoff">— Reno Sportscaster Booth, edited for length</div>
      </article>
